Lemma 6.37
.

For
\varepsilon>0
,
c_{0}>0
,
\frac{1}{\eta_{0}}>0
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
T>0
and any
C_{0}=C_{0}(c_{0},\frac{1}{\eta_{0}},N,T)
and
C_{0,1,2,\tau}(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)=C_{0,1,2,\tau}(c_{0},\frac{1}{\eta_{0}},N,T,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
from Notations
4.5
and
6.21
, there exist
\lambda_{0}>0
,
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
, such that for any
0<\lambda<\lambda_{0}
and for any
0\leq\tau\leq T
we have

\displaystyle C_{0}\frac{c_{1}}{c_{2}}e^{\tau c_{2}}+C_{0}+C_{0,1,2,\tau}(c_{1},c_{2},\tau)\lambda^{\varepsilon}\leq\frac{1}{2}c_{1}e^{\tau c_{2}}.

Proof.

We first choose
c_{2}
sufficiently large so that
\frac{C_{0}}{c_{2}}\leq\frac{1}{6}
. Next, we choose a sufficiently large
c_{1}
so that
C_{0}\leq\frac{1}{6}c_{1}
. Finally, we choose
\lambda_{0}
such that
C_{0,1,2,\tau}(c_{1},c_{2},T)\lambda^{\varepsilon}\leq\frac{1}{6}c_{1}
.
∎

6.4
Exact solutions to KGML

We reformulate more precisely and prove the rough Proposition
6.2
.

Proposition 6.38
.

For a given admissible background initial data set
(a^{\alpha}_{0},\dot{a}^{\alpha}_{0},\phi_{0},\dot{\phi}_{0},v_{\mathscr{A}},\dot{v}_{\mathscr{A}},\psi_{\mathscr{A}},w^{\alpha}_{\mathscr{A}})
, let
\lambda>0
and let
((e^{evo}_{\lambda})^{\alpha},\epsilon^{evo}_{\lambda},(\dot{e}^{evo}_{\lambda})^{\alpha},\dot{\epsilon}^{evo}_{\lambda},w^{+\alpha}_{\lambda\mathscr{A}},\breve{w}^{+\alpha}_{\mathscr{A}\pm\mathscr{B}},\psi^{+}_{\lambda\mathscr{A}},\breve{\psi}^{+}_{\mathscr{A}\pm\mathscr{B}},g^{+\alpha}_{W^{+}_{\lambda\mathscr{A}}},g^{+}_{\Psi^{+}_{\lambda\mathscr{A}}},(e^{evo}_{\lambda})^{\alpha},\epsilon^{ell}_{\lambda},(\dot{e}^{ell}_{\lambda})^{\alpha},\dot{\epsilon}^{ell}_{\lambda})
be error initial data
admissible for KGML
, with the associated
-3/2<\delta<-1/2
, from Definition
5.10
. Then, for any
\kappa>0
, determining the projectors from Definition
2.5
, there exists a time
t_{\lambda}>0
for which there exists a local exact multiphase high-frequency solution to KGML
(A^{\alpha}_{\lambda},\Phi_{\lambda})
(
1.12
) corresponding to these initial data assembled as in Definition
5.5
. Moreover,

\displaystyle A^{0}_{\lambda}=A^{0}_{1\lambda}+Z_{\lambda}^{0}\in\bigcap_{j=0}^{2}C^{j}([0,t_{\lambda}],H^{2-j}_{\delta+j}),

\displaystyle A^{i}_{\lambda}=A^{i}_{1\lambda}+Z_{\lambda}^{i}\in\bigcap_{j=0}^{2}C^{j}([0,t_{\lambda}],H^{2-j}),

\displaystyle\Phi_{\lambda}=\Phi_{1\lambda}+\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}\in\bigcap_{j=0}^{2}C^{j}([0,t_{\lambda}],H^{2-j}),

where
(A^{\alpha}_{1\lambda},\Phi_{1\lambda})
are almost approximate solutions of order 1 to KGM in Lorenz gauge, and where
(Z_{\lambda}^{\alpha},\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda})
is the precise error term given by

\displaystyle Z_{\lambda}^{\alpha}=\sum_{\mathscr{A}\in\mathbb{A}}\lambda^{1}\Re\left(e^{i\frac{u_{\mathscr{A}}}{\lambda}}\overline{W^{+\alpha}_{\lambda\mathscr{A}}}\right)+\sum_{(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{B})\in\mathscr{C}}\lambda^{1}\Re\left(e^{i\frac{u_{\mathscr{A}}\pm u_{\mathscr{B}}}{\lambda}}\overline{\breve{W}^{+\alpha}_{\mathscr{A}\pm\mathscr{B}}}\right)+(E_{\lambda}^{evo})^{\alpha}+(E_{\lambda}^{ell})^{\alpha},

(6.55)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}=\sum_{\mathscr{A}\in\mathbb{A}}\lambda^{1}\Psi^{+}_{\lambda\mathscr{A}}e^{i\frac{u_{\mathscr{A}}}{\lambda}}+\sum_{(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{B})\in\mathscr{C}}\lambda^{1}\breve{\Psi}^{+}_{\mathscr{A}\pm\mathscr{B}}e^{i\frac{u_{\mathscr{A}}\pm u_{\mathscr{B}}}{\lambda}}+\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}^{evo}+\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}^{ell}.

(6.56)

Proof.

The almost approximate solution
(A^{\alpha}_{1\lambda},\Phi_{1\lambda})
is constructed in Proposition
4.1
. Then, we argue schematically. The existence of
\boldsymbol{\breve{F}}^{+}_{\mathscr{A}\pm\mathscr{B}}
is given by Proposition
6.10
and that of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}^{ell}
by Definition
6.12
, both defined on
[0,T]
. For initial data admissible for KGML, there exists a time
t_{\lambda}
for which there exists a solution
(\textbf{F}^{+}_{\lambda\mathscr{A}},\textbf{G}^{+}_{\lambda\mathscr{A}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}^{evo})
to the system (
6.5
), by Proposition
6.17
. Using Remarks
6.9
,
6.14
,
6.24
,
6.26
and
6.29
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\textbf{Z}_{\lambda}=\sum_{\mathscr{A}\in\mathbb{A}}\lambda^{1}\textbf{F}^{+}_{\lambda\mathscr{A}}e^{i\frac{u_{\mathscr{A}}}{\lambda}}+\sum_{(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{B})\in\mathscr{C}}\lambda^{1}\breve{\textbf{F}}^{+}_{\mathscr{A}\pm\mathscr{B}}e^{i\frac{u_{\mathscr{A}}\pm u_{\mathscr{B}}}{\lambda}}+\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}^{ell}+\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}^{evo}

is a solution to (
6.5
). By construction, the equation (
6.5
) depends on the background from Proposition
4.1
and is designed so that, if
\textbf{Z}_{\lambda}
solves (
6.5
), then
\textbf{F}_{\lambda}=\textbf{F}_{1\lambda}+\textbf{Z}_{\lambda}
solves (
3.1
), i.e.,
(A_{\lambda}=A_{1\lambda}+Z_{\lambda},\Phi_{\lambda}=\Phi_{1\lambda}+\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda})
is a solution to KGML (
1.12
). The regularity follows directly from Propositions
6.10
,
6.15
, and
6.17
.
∎

6.5
Uniform existence time in
\lambda

We reformulate more precisely and prove the rough Proposition
6.4
.

Proposition 6.39
.

Under the assumption of Proposition
6.38
, if the error initial data satisfy the required smallness condition in the sense of Definition
5.13
, then there exist
\kappa_{0}>0
, determining the projectors from Definition
2.5
, and
\lambda_{0}>0
such that
t_{\lambda}=T
for
0<\lambda<\lambda_{0}
, i.e., the family of solutions
(A^{\alpha}_{\lambda},\Phi_{\lambda})_{0<\lambda<\lambda_{0}}
to (
1.12
) given in Proposition
6.38
exists on the interval
[0,T]
.

Proof.

This follows from a standard bootstrap argument. We assume that the estimates (
6.25
) of the bootstrap Assumptions
6.20
hold for some
0<c_{1}<c_{2}
sufficiently large. Then, by Lemma
6.37
, there exists
0<\lambda_{0}
sufficiently small such that the estimates (
6.25
) hold with
c_{1}
replaced by
\frac{1}{2}c_{1}
. The bootstrap is initialised using Proposition
6.38
. This implies that
t_{\lambda}=T
for
0<\lambda<\lambda_{0}
.
∎

6.6
Exact solutions to KGM in Lorenz gauge

We reformulate more precisely and prove the rough Proposition
6.5
.

Proposition 6.40
.

Under the assumption of Proposition
6.38
, if the error initial data are
KGM in Lorenz gauge admissible
in the sense of Definition
5.12
, then the solution
(A_{\lambda},\Phi_{\lambda})
to KGML (
1.12
) given by Proposition
6.38
is also a solution to KGM (
1.1
) in Lorenz gauge. For all
t\in[0,t_{\lambda}]
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}A^{\alpha}_{\lambda}(t)=0,

(6.57)

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}A^{\alpha}_{0}(t)=0,

(6.58)

\displaystyle\forall\mathscr{A}\in\mathbb{A},\;\partial_{\alpha}u_{\mathscr{A}}W^{\alpha}_{\mathscr{A}},(t)=0,

(6.59)

and, if the components are initially polarized as

\displaystyle\forall\mathscr{A}\in\mathbb{A},\;\partial_{\alpha}u_{\mathscr{A}}w^{+\alpha}_{\lambda\mathscr{A}}=0,

\displaystyle\forall(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{B})\in\mathscr{C},\;(\partial_{\alpha}u_{\mathscr{A}}\pm\partial_{\alpha}u_{\mathscr{B}})\breve{w}^{+\alpha}_{\mathscr{A}\pm\mathscr{B}}=0,

(6.60)

then

\displaystyle\forall\mathscr{A}\in\mathbb{A},\;\partial_{\alpha}u_{\mathscr{A}}W^{+\alpha}_{\lambda\mathscr{A}},(t)=0,

\displaystyle\forall(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{B})\in\mathscr{C},\;(\partial_{\alpha}u_{\mathscr{A}}\pm\partial_{\alpha}u_{\mathscr{B}})\breve{W}^{+\alpha}_{\mathscr{A}\pm\mathscr{B}}(t)=0,

(6.61)

and

\begin{split}&\sum_{\mathscr{A}\in\mathbb{A}}\left(\lambda^{1/2}\Re\left(e^{i\frac{u_{\mathscr{A}}}{\lambda}}\overline{\partial_{\alpha}W_{\mathscr{A}}^{\alpha}}\right)+\lambda^{1}\Re\left(e^{i\frac{u_{\mathscr{A}}}{\lambda}}\overline{\partial_{\alpha}W^{+\alpha}_{\lambda\mathscr{A}}}\right)\right)(t)+\sum_{(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{B})\in\mathscr{C}}\lambda^{1}\Re\left(e^{i\frac{u_{\mathscr{A}}\pm u_{\mathscr{B}}}{\lambda}}\overline{\partial_{\alpha}\breve{W}_{\mathscr{A}\pm\mathscr{B}}^{+\alpha}}\right)(t)\\
&+\partial_{\alpha}(E_{\lambda A}^{ell})^{\alpha}(t)+\partial_{\alpha}(E_{\lambda A}^{evo})^{\alpha}(t)=0.\end{split}

(6.62)

Proof.

Since KGM in Lorenz gauge admissible initial data satisfy the constraints, the propagation of gauge follows directly from the argument of Section
1.1.1
.

For the decomposition of the divergence of
A_{\lambda}
, identities (
6.57
) and (
6.58
) follow from (
4.3
) and (
4.4
). For the propagation of polarization, Remark
6.23
shows that the RHS of the evolution equation for
W^{+\alpha}_{\lambda\mathscr{A}}
(resp.
\breve{W}^{+\alpha}_{\mathscr{A}\pm\mathscr{B}}
) is orthogonal to
\partial_{\alpha}u_{\mathscr{A}}
(resp.
\partial_{\beta}u_{\mathscr{A}}\pm\partial_{\beta}u_{\mathscr{B}}
). Thus, it follows from direct computations that
\mathscr{L}_{\mathscr{A}}(W^{+\alpha}_{\lambda\mathscr{A}}\partial_{\alpha}u_{\mathscr{A}})=0
(resp.

\mathscr{L}_{\mathscr{A}\pm\mathscr{B}}(\breve{W}^{+\alpha}_{\mathscr{A}\pm\mathscr{B}}(\partial_{\beta}u_{\mathscr{A}}\pm\partial_{\beta}u_{\mathscr{B}}))=0
). Finally, the last identity is obtained by collecting the remaining terms.
∎

Remark 6.41
.

The set we exhibit in Proposition
5.17
is polarized, in the sense that
\breve{w}^{+\alpha}_{\mathscr{A}\pm\mathscr{B}}=w^{+\alpha}_{\lambda\mathscr{A}}=0
.

7
Putting everything together

We prove Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
and conclude with several general remarks.

7.1
Proof of the Theorems

For the first Theorem
3.1
, we only detail the proof of point
1
since the remaining statements follow directly from our construction. The point
1
asserts that, for a given admissible initial ansatz, there exists
\lambda_{0}>0
such that there is a family of multiphase high-frequency solutions
(A_{\lambda},\Phi_{\lambda})_{0<\lambda<\lambda_{0}}
to KGM (
1.1
) on
[0,T]
. Following the strategy outlined in Section
3.2
, we first construct the first-order approximate solution (step
(i)
) using Proposition
4.1
. Then, we construct initial data for the error that are admissible in the sense of Definitions
5.10
,
5.13
and
5.12
using Proposition
5.17
(step
(ii)
). Next, we construct a family of exact solutions
(A_{\lambda},\Phi_{\lambda})_{0<\lambda}
to KGML (
1.12
) associated with the error initial data, each solution being defined on
[0,t_{\lambda}]
(step
(iii)
). Using Proposition
6.36
, we exhibit
0<\lambda_{0}
such that the sub-family
(A_{\lambda},\Phi_{\lambda})_{0<\lambda<\lambda_{0}}
exists on the uniform interval
[0,T]
(step
(iv)
). Finally, we show that the gauge condition propagates and that the family
(A_{\lambda},\Phi_{\lambda})_{0<\lambda<\lambda_{0}}
indeed solves KGM (
1.1
) while satisfying the Lorenz gauge condition (step
(v)
). As a consequence, we establish the stability of the multiphase WKB analysis applied to KGM in Lorenz gauge, using only a first-order approximate solution and under low regularity assumptions.

For the second Theorem, the proof is direct from the structure of the solution described above. In particular, the
L^{p}([0,T],L^{\infty})
-convergence is obtained via the Strichartz inequality for waves, as presented in Theorem
, while the fact that the limit
(A_{0},\Phi_{0})
is a solution to (
1.7
) follows from the construction of the approximate solution in Proposition
4.1
. This provides an explicit manifestation of a backreaction phenomenon in the high-frequency limit of solutions to KGM.

7.2
Comparison with the literature

It is instructive to compare our results with those of
[
15
]
. The author of the latter constructs monophase high-frequency solutions to the Yang-Mills-Higgs-Dirac system, which includes the KGM system (
1.1
). More precisely, approximate solutions are constructed at arbitrary order using a WKB expansion of the form

\displaystyle\textbf{F}_{M\lambda}=\sum_{0\leq i\leq M-1}\lambda^{i/2}\left(\tilde{\textbf{F}}_{i}(x)+\lambda^{1/2}\overset{\star}{\textbf{F}}_{i}\left(x,\frac{u}{\lambda}\right)\right)

together with exact solutions

\displaystyle\textbf{F}_{\lambda}=\textbf{F}_{M\lambda}+\textbf{Z}_{\lambda},

constructed on a time interval uniform in
\lambda
, provided that
M\geq n
where
n
denotes the spatial dimension. The functions
\textbf{F}_{i}(x,.)
are decomposed into a mean part
\tilde{\textbf{F}}_{i}(x)
and a purely oscillatory part
\overset{\star}{\textbf{F}}_{i}(x,.)
. The oscillatory profiles involve general harmonics rather than being restricted to
\cos(\frac{u}{\lambda})
and
\sin(\frac{u}{\lambda})
. By contrast, in our construction, harmonics do not appear at the level of the (first-order) approximate solution

\displaystyle\textbf{F}_{1\lambda}=\tilde{\textbf{F}}_{0}(x)+\lambda^{1/2}\overset{\star}{\textbf{F}}_{0}\left(x,\frac{u}{\lambda}\right):=\textbf{F}_{0}(x)+\lambda^{1/2}\textbf{F}(x)e^{i\frac{u}{\lambda}}.

and our method naturally accommodates multiphase superpositions.

In
[
15
]
, the following bounds are obtained for the Yang-Mills potential and the scalar field (corresponding to
A_{\lambda}
and
\Phi_{\lambda}
in the KGM system):

\displaystyle\forall\lambda<\lambda_{0},\;\lambda^{M/2}||\textbf{F}_{\lambda}||_{H^{1/2}}+||\textbf{Z}_{\lambda}||_{H^{1/2}}\leq\lambda^{M/2}C,

for some constant
C
depending on the initial data. Formally extrapolating these estimates to the case
M=1
, which is excluded in
[
15
]
, corresponds to the bounds obtained in Theorem
3.1
. In the present work, we construct the error term already at order
M=1
, that is, below the dimensional threshold
M=3
.
In
[
15
]
, the obstruction to this low-order construction originates from the use of specific product estimates (Proposition 3.1.1 in
[
15
]
and 2.2.2 in
[
14
]
). This obstruction is consistent with the more general results of
[
30
]
for first-order hyperbolic systems. Higher-order approximate solutions typically require the construction of a large hierarchical system of equations, as well as a detailed analysis of harmonics and phase interactions arising from nonlinear effects. Our first-order construction bypasses these difficulties.

7.3
Regularity of the background

The background
\textbf{B}^{\prime}_{0}:=(A_{0},\Phi_{0},w_{\mathscr{A}},\psi_{\mathscr{A}},\textbf{d}u_{\mathscr{A}})
defined in Proposition
4.1
is more regular than the full solution (and, in particular, the error term). This additional regularity is mandatory for the well-posedness of (
6.23
) and (
6.12
), as well as for the estimates carried out in Section
6.3.1
. For example, the equation satisfied by
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}^{evo}
contains the source term
\Box\textbf{F}_{\mathscr{A}}
, which formally leads to the regularity relation
\partial\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{\lambda}^{evo}\sim\Box\textbf{F}_{\mathscr{A}}
. Consequently,
\textbf{B}_{0}=(A_{0},\Phi_{0},w_{\mathscr{A}},\psi_{\mathscr{A}})
must be one degree more regular than the error term. Moreover, the phases satisfy the regularity bound

\displaystyle\max_{\mathscr{A}\in\mathbb{A}}\sum_{j=0}^{5}||u_{\mathscr{A}}||_{C^{j}([0,T],H^{5-j}_{{\delta_{1}}+j}}\leq c_{0}.

This level of regularity is required to ensure the well-posedness of (
4.1
) with solutions in
H^{3}
and to be able to use the commutator estimates of Proposition
8.6
in the analysis of (
6.33
). In fact, it would suffice to assume

\Box u_{\mathscr{A}}
in
H^{3}
and
u_{\mathscr{A}}
in
H^{4}_{\delta_{1}}
. Constructing the error term for higher-order approximations would require an even more regular background.

8
Appendix

8.1
Estimates for evolution equations

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
. For a function
f
defined on
[0,T^{\prime}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, for some
T^{\prime}>0
, solution to

\begin{cases}\Box f=h,\\
f(t=0)=f_{0}\in H^{m+1}_{\delta^{\prime}-1}\;\;\partial_{t}f(t=0)=f_{1}\in H^{m}_{\delta^{\prime}},\end{cases}

(8.1)

the following energy estimates for
\partial f=(\partial_{t}f,\nabla f)
holds

\displaystyle||\partial f(t)||_{H^{m}_{\delta^{\prime}}}\leq C(||\partial f(0)||_{H^{m}_{\delta^{\prime}}}+\int_{0}^{t}||h(s)||_{H^{m}_{\delta^{\prime}}}+||\partial f(s)||_{H^{m}_{\delta^{\prime}}}ds).

(8.2)

Moreover, in the case
\delta^{\prime}=0
,

\displaystyle||\partial f(t)||_{H^{m}}\leq C(||\partial f(0)||_{H^{m}}+\int_{0}^{t}||h(s)||_{H^{m}}ds).

(8.3)

Proof.

We present the estimate for
m=0
and for any
{\delta^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}
. Multiplying the equation by
(1+|x|^{2})^{\delta^{\prime}}\partial_{t}f
gives

\displaystyle\Box f(1+|x|^{2})^{\delta^{\prime}}\partial_{t}f=h(1+|x|^{2})^{\delta^{\prime}}\partial_{t}f.

Integrating over
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Box f(1+|x|^{2})^{\delta^{\prime}}\partial_{t}fdx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}h(1+|x|^{2})^{\delta^{\prime}}\partial_{t}fdx

\displaystyle=-\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(1+|x|^{2})^{\delta^{\prime}}\frac{\partial_{t}f^{2}+|\nabla f|^{2}}{2}dx-2\delta^{\prime}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(1+|x|^{2})^{\delta^{\prime}-1}x_{i}\partial_{i}f\partial_{t}fdx.

Since
|(1+|x|^{2})^{\delta^{\prime}-1}x_{i}|\leq(1+|x|^{2})^{\delta^{\prime}}
, the second term can be controlled by the
L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}}
norm. Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\frac{1}{2}||\partial f(t)||^{2}_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}}}\leq C(||\partial f(t)||_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}}}||h(t)||_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}}}+||\partial f(t)||^{2}_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}}}),

and so,

\displaystyle||\partial f(t)||_{L^{2}_{\delta}}\leq C(||\partial f(0)||_{L^{2}_{\delta}}+\int_{0}^{t}||h(s)||_{L^{2}_{\delta}}+||\partial f(s)||_{L^{2}_{\delta}}ds).

The general case follows by commuting the equation with
\partial_{\overrightarrow{m}}
where
\overrightarrow{m}
is a multi-index with
|\overrightarrow{m}|=m
, multiplying by
\partial_{t\overrightarrow{m}}f
instead of
\partial_{t}f
, and using the adjusted weight.

∎

Moreover, bounds on
\partial f(t)
together with bounds on
f_{0}
yield bounds on
f(t)
for finite time, using

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}||f(t)||_{L^{2}}\leq||\partial_{t}f(t)||_{L^{2}}.

(8.4)

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
f
be a solution to (
8.1
). Then, for all
t\in[0,T^{\prime}]
,
\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
and for every
m\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle||f(t)||_{H^{m+1}_{\delta^{\prime}-1}}\leq(||f_{0}||_{H^{m+1}_{\delta^{\prime}-1}}+t\sup_{s\in[0,T^{\prime}]}||\partial f(s))||_{H^{m}_{\delta^{\prime}}}).

(8.5)

Proof.

The control of the
H^{m}_{\delta^{\prime}-1}
norm of
f
is obtained by integrating (
8.4
) for higher-order weighted Sobolev norms. The control of the
(m+1)
-th derivatives of
f
in
L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+m}
follows directly from the bound on
||\partial f||_{H^{m}_{\delta^{\prime}}}
.

∎

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
f
be a solution to (
8.1
). Then, for all
t\in[0,T^{\prime}]
,
\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
,
and for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the time derivatives of
f
obey the following inequalities

\displaystyle||\partial_{t}f(t)||_{H^{m}_{\delta^{\prime}}}\leq C(||\partial f(0)||_{H^{m}_{\delta^{\prime}}}+\int_{0}^{t}||h(s)||_{H^{m}_{\delta^{\prime}}}+||\partial f(s)||_{H^{m}_{\delta^{\prime}}}ds),

(8.6)

and if
m\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}

\displaystyle||\partial_{tt}^{2}f(t)||_{H^{m-1}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}\leq(||f(t)||_{H^{m+1}_{\delta^{\prime}-1}}+||h(t)||_{H^{m-1}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}).

(8.7)

Proof.

The first inequality is a specific case of (
8.2
) and the second follows directly using the wave equation in (
8.1
).
∎

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
. For a function
f
defined on
[0,T^{\prime}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, for some
T^{\prime}>0
, solution to

\begin{cases}2\partial^{\alpha}u\partial_{\alpha}f+f\Box u=j,\\
f(t=0)=f_{0}\in H^{m},\end{cases}

(8.8)

with
u
a smooth characteristic phase satisfying

\displaystyle\underset{(t,x)\in[0,T^{\prime}]\times Supp(f)}{\min}|\partial_{t}u(t,x)|>\eta,

\displaystyle||\partial u||_{H^{m^{\prime}}}+||\Box u||_{H^{m^{\prime}}}+||\partial_{t}\partial u||_{H^{m^{\prime}-1}}+||\partial_{t}\Box u||_{H^{m^{\prime}-1}}\leq C_{u},

(8.9)

for some
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{\star}_{+}
, some
C_{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{\star}_{+}
and for
m^{\prime}=\max(m,2)
, the following energy estimate holds

\displaystyle||f||_{L^{\infty}([0,T^{\prime}],H^{m})}\leq C_{u}(||f(0)||_{H^{m}}+\int_{0}^{T^{\prime}}||j(s)||_{H^{m}}+||f(s)||_{H^{m}}ds).

(8.10)

Proof.

In the case
m=0
, we multiply (
8.8
) by
f

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}(\partial^{\alpha}u_{\mathscr{A}}f^{2})=jf,

which yields a conservative transport equation. By Stokes’ Theorem
19
19

19

Boundary terms are ignored since they play no role in our application.
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\partial^{0}u_{\mathscr{A}}f^{2})(t)dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(\partial^{0}u_{\mathscr{A}}f^{2})(0)dx+\int^{t}_{0}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(jf)(s)dx}ds,

so that, using the lower bounds on
|\partial_{t}u|
,

\displaystyle\eta||f(t)||_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C_{u}||f(0)||_{L^{2}}^{2}+||f||_{L^{\infty}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{2})}\int^{t}_{0}{||j(s)||_{L^{2}}}ds,

which implies

\displaystyle||f||_{L^{\infty}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{2})}\leq C_{u,\eta}(||f(0)||_{L^{2}}+\int^{T^{\prime}}_{0}{||j(s)||_{L^{2}}}ds).

For
m\geq 1
, commuting the equation with
\partial_{\overrightarrow{m}}
, where
\overrightarrow{m}
is a multi-index with
|\overrightarrow{m}|=m
, yields

\displaystyle(2\partial^{\alpha}u\partial_{\alpha}+\Box u)\partial_{\overrightarrow{m}}f=[2\partial^{\alpha}u\partial_{\alpha}+\Box u,\partial_{\overrightarrow{m}}]f+\partial_{\overrightarrow{m}}j,

where

\displaystyle||[2\partial^{\alpha}u\partial_{\alpha}+\Box u,\partial_{\overrightarrow{m}}]f(t)||_{L^{2}}\leq C(||\partial u(t)||_{H^{m^{\prime}}}+||\Box u(t)||_{H^{m^{\prime}}})||f(t)||_{H^{m}},

for
m^{\prime}=\max(m,2)
and a universal constant
C\in\mathbb{R}^{\star}_{+}
.
∎

Proposition 8.5
.

Let
f
be a solution to (
8.8
). Then, for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the time derivatives of
f
obey the following inequalities

\displaystyle||\partial_{t}f||_{L^{\infty}([0,T^{\prime}],H^{m})}\leq C_{u,\eta}(||f||_{L^{\infty}([0,T^{\prime}],H^{m+1})}+||j||_{L^{\infty}([0,T^{\prime}],H^{m})}),

(8.11)

\displaystyle||\partial^{2}_{tt}f||_{L^{\infty}([0,T^{\prime}],H^{m-1})}\leq C_{u,\eta}(||f||_{L^{\infty}([0,T^{\prime}],H^{m+1})}+||j||_{L^{\infty}([0,T^{\prime}],H^{m})}).

(8.12)

Proof.

The first estimate follows directly from the transport equation.
The second follows by differentiating the transport equation in time and using the previous estimate to control the appearing first-order time derivatives.
∎

8.2
Commutator

Proposition 8.6
.

Let
g
be a smooth Lorentzian metric. We define
\Box:=D_{\alpha}D^{\alpha}
, where
D
denotes the covariant derivative and indices are contracted with respect to
g
. Let
f
, defined on
[0,T^{\prime}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, be compactly supported and solution to

\begin{cases}2\partial^{\alpha}u\partial_{\alpha}f+\Box uf=j,\\
f(t=0)=f_{0}\in H^{2},\end{cases}

(8.13)

for
u
a smooth characteristic phase such that, for every compact set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
, there exists
C_{u,\Omega}
such that

\displaystyle||\Box\partial u||_{L^{\infty}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{\infty}(\Omega))}+||\Box\Box u||_{L^{\infty}([0,T^{\prime}],H^{1/2}(\Omega))}+||\partial\partial u||_{L^{\infty}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{\infty}(\Omega))}\leq C_{u,\Omega}.

(8.14)

Assume that

(\Box f)|_{t=0}=G_{0},\;G_{0}\in H^{1}.

(8.15)

Let
G
, defined on
[0,T^{\prime}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, be solution to

\begin{cases}2\partial^{\alpha}u\partial_{\alpha}G+\Box uG=\Box j+[(2\partial^{\alpha}u\partial_{\alpha}+\Box u),\Box]f,\\
G(t=0)=G_{0}.\end{cases}

(8.16)

Then,
G
belongs to
H^{1}
for all
t\in[0,T^{\prime}]
and

||G(t)||_{H^{1}}\leq C_{g,u,\Omega}\int^{t}_{0}(||\Box j(s)||_{H^{1}}+||\partial j(s)||_{H^{1}}+||f(s)||_{H^{2}})ds+||G_{0}||_{H^{1}},

(8.17)

where we use the pointwise-in-time estimate

||[(2\partial^{\alpha}u\partial_{\alpha}+\Box u),\Box]f(t)||_{L^{2}}\leq C_{g,u}(||G(t)||_{L^{2}}+||\partial j(t)||_{L^{2}}+||f(t)||_{H^{1}}).

(8.18)

Proof.

Firstly, if
u=k^{\alpha}x_{\alpha}
for
k
a constant vector, that is, if
u
is the phase of a plane wave, then
\partial_{\alpha}u=k_{\alpha}
so that

[(2\partial^{\alpha}u\partial_{\alpha}+\Box u),\Box]=[(2k^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}),\Box]=0.

(8.19)

The result follows immediately. We now treat the general case. For simplicity, we work in wave coordinates satisfying
g^{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\gamma}_{\mu\nu}=0
. Then

\displaystyle[(2\partial^{\alpha}u\partial_{\alpha}+\Box u),\Box]f

\displaystyle=2\partial^{\alpha}u\partial_{\alpha}(g^{\mu\nu})\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}f-2\partial^{\alpha}u\partial_{\alpha}(g^{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\gamma}_{\mu\nu})\partial_{\gamma}f-4\Box\partial^{\alpha}u\partial_{\alpha}f-4g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\alpha}u\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\alpha}f-\Box\Box uf

\displaystyle+g^{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\gamma}_{\mu\nu}2\partial^{\alpha}\partial_{\gamma}u\partial_{\alpha}+g^{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\gamma}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\gamma}\Box u

\displaystyle=-4(\Gamma^{\mu}_{\rho\alpha}g^{\rho\nu}\partial^{\alpha}u+g^{\mu\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\partial^{\nu}u)\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}f-4(\Box\partial^{\alpha}u)\partial_{\alpha}f-\Box\Box uf

\displaystyle=-4(\nabla^{\mu}\partial^{\nu}u)\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}f-4(\Box\partial^{\alpha}u)\partial_{\alpha}f-\Box\Box uf.

We decompose the metric as

g^{\mu\nu}=-\frac{1}{2}(\partial^{\mu}u\partial^{\nu}\bar{u}+\partial^{\mu}\bar{u}\partial^{\nu}u)+\delta^{MN}e_{M}^{\mu}e_{N}^{\nu},

where
e_{M,N}
are spacelike vector fields orthonormal to
\partial u
. The vector field
\partial\bar{u}
is the unique null vector satisfying
g(\partial u,\partial\bar{u})=-2
and
g(E_{M},\partial\bar{u})=0
.

We know that
\partial_{\nu}uD^{\mu}\partial^{\nu}u=0
(
2.2
) and
\partial_{\nu}uD^{\nu}\partial^{\mu}u=0
(
2.5
). Therefore, locally, the tensor
\nabla^{\mu}\partial^{\nu}u
can be written as

a\partial u\otimes\partial u+b(\partial u\otimes e_{M,N}+e_{M,N}\otimes\partial u)+c(e_{M,N}\otimes e_{M,N}),

a
,
b
and
c
depend on
\partial\partial u
and
\partial g
, while the other components vanish.
Hence, schematically

[(2\partial^{\alpha}u\partial_{\alpha}+\Box u),\Box]f=f_{1}(\partial\partial u,\partial g)\partial(\partial^{\alpha}u\partial_{\alpha}f)+f_{2}(\partial\partial u,\partial g)\partial f+f_{3}(\partial\partial u,\partial g)\partial_{M}\partial_{N}f+\Box\partial u\partial f+\Box\Box uf,

for suitable coefficient functions
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}
.

The tangential Hessian
\partial_{M}\partial_{N}f
can be estimated in
L^{2}
on surfaces of constant
u
and
\bar{u}
via
\Delta_{M,N}f
and
\partial f
, see
[
34
]
.
Using the identity

\Delta_{M,N}f=\Box f+\frac{1}{2}(\partial^{\mu}u\partial^{\nu}\bar{u}+\partial^{\mu}\bar{u}\partial^{\nu}u)\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}f,

we obtain

||\partial_{M}\partial_{N}f||_{L^{2}}\leq C_{u,g}(||\Box f||_{L^{2}}+||\partial(\partial^{\alpha}u\partial_{\alpha}f)||_{L^{2}}+||\partial f||_{L^{2}}).

Putting everything together, we deduce

||[(2\partial^{\alpha}u\partial_{\alpha}+\Box u),\Box]f(t)||_{L^{2}}\leq C_{g,u,\Omega}(||\Box f||_{L^{2}}+||\partial(\partial^{\alpha}u\partial_{\alpha}f)||_{L^{2}}+||f||_{H^{1}})=C_{u,g}(||G||_{L^{2}}+||\partial j||_{L^{2}}+||f||_{H^{1}}).

This is the desired inequality.
∎

8.3
Sobolev inequalities

In this Section, we recall classical Sobolev embedding and interpolation results.

Proposition 8.7
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
be the considered dimension. Let
k>0
,
1\leq p<\infty
,
1\leq q<\infty
,
k>l
and
\frac{1}{p}\geq\frac{1}{q}\geq\frac{1}{p}-\frac{k-l}{n}
. Then

W^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow W^{l,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Proof.

From
[
2
]
.
∎

Proposition 8.8
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
be the considered dimension. Let
k>0
,
1\leq p<\infty
,
1\leq q<\infty
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\geq\frac{1}{q}\geq\frac{1}{p}-\frac{k}{n}\geq 0

\displaystyle\implies W^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}-\frac{k}{n}<0

\displaystyle\implies W^{k,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Proof.

From
[
2
]
.
∎

Proposition 8.9
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
be the considered dimension. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
1\leq p\leq\infty
, and
\infty>s>n/p
. For weights
\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}
satisfying
\delta^{\prime}\leq\delta^{\prime\prime}+\frac{n}{p}
, we have the embedding of weighted Sobolev spaces
W^{s+m,p}_{\delta^{\prime\prime}}
into the weighted Hölder spaces
C^{m}_{\delta^{\prime}}
:

\displaystyle W^{s+m,p}_{\delta^{\prime\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow C^{m}_{\delta^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Proof.

From
[
4
]
.
∎

Proposition 8.10
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
be the considered dimension. For

s_{\theta},s_{0},s_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
,

1\leq p_{\theta},p_{0},p_{1}\leq\infty
and
0<\theta<1
with

\frac{1}{p_{\theta}}=\frac{1-\theta}{p_{0}}+\frac{\theta}{p_{1}}
and
s_{\theta}=(1-\theta)s_{0}+(\theta)s_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle||f||_{W^{s_{\theta},p_{\theta}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq(||f||_{W^{s_{0},p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})})^{1-\theta}(||f||_{W^{s_{1},p_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})})^{\theta}.

(8.20)

Proof.

From
[
2
]
.
∎

Proposition 8.11

(Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
be the considered dimension.
Let
1\leq r,q\leq\infty
,
j,m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
j<m
and
p\geq 1
, then for

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}=\frac{j}{n}+\theta\left(\frac{1}{r}-\frac{m}{n}\right)+(1-\theta)\frac{1}{q},\;\;\frac{j}{m}\leq\theta\leq 1,

there exists some
C>0
such that

\displaystyle||\partial^{j}_{x}f||_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C(||\partial^{m}_{x}f||_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})})^{\theta}(||f||_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})})^{1-\theta}.

Proof.

From
[
2
]
.
∎

8.4
Laplacian inversion

Definition 8.12
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
be the considered dimension. For
1<p<\infty
,
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the classical Laplacian as
\Delta=\partial_{i}\partial^{i}
where

\Delta:W^{2+m,p}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\xrightarrow{}W^{m,p}_{\delta+2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Theorem 8.1
.

For
1<p<\infty
and
n\geq 3
, we set
p^{\prime}=1-1/p
. Then, for
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\delta\not\equiv-2+\frac{n}{p^{\prime}}\mod(\mathbb{N})
and
-\delta\not\equiv\frac{n}{p}\mod(\mathbb{N})
with

\displaystyle-n/p<\delta<-2+n/p^{\prime},

the Laplacian operator is an isomorphism from
W^{2+m,p}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
to
W^{m,p}_{\delta+2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and is therefore invertible.

Moreover, there exists
C>0
such that for any
h\in L^{2}_{\delta+2}
and for
f\in H^{2}_{\delta}
the solution to

-\Delta f=h,

we have

||f||_{H^{2}_{\delta}}\leq C||h||_{L^{2}_{\delta+2}}.

(8.21)

Proof.

From
[
29
]
.
∎

Remark 8.13
.

In the case
n=3
,
p=2
and
p^{\prime}=2
, we obtain

-3/2<\delta<-1/2
.
For
m=0
, the source term must satisfy
h\in L^{2}_{\delta+2}\subset{L^{2}}
(since
\delta+2>0
). If
Supp(h)\subset{K}
for
K
compact, then

||h||_{L^{2}_{\delta+2}}\leq C||h||_{L^{2}(K)}.

We deduce the following useful Lemma.

Lemma 8.14
.

Let

L=-\Delta+g,

(8.22)

where
g\geq 0
is a compactly supported function in
H^{2}
satisfying
||g||_{H^{2}}\leq C_{g}
. Then, for
\delta\in(-3/2,-1/2)
, the operator
L
is an isomorphism from
H^{2}_{\delta}
to
L^{2}_{\delta+2}
and is therefore invertible.

Moreover, there exists
C>0
such that for any
h\in L^{2}_{\delta+2}
and for
f\in H^{2}_{\delta}
the solution to

Lf=h,

(8.23)

we have

||f||_{H^{2}_{\delta}}\leq C||h||_{L^{2}_{\delta+2}}.

(8.24)

Proof.

First, we prove that the multiplication by
g
defines a compact operator from
H^{2}_{\delta}
to
L^{2}_{\delta+2}
.

Let
B
be a ball of finite radius such that
Supp(g)\subset B
. For
f\in H^{2}_{\delta}
with
||f||_{H^{2}_{\delta}}\leq C
, we have

\displaystyle||gf||_{H^{1}(B)}\leq||g||_{H^{2}(B)}||f||_{H^{2}(B)}\leq c(B)||f||_{H^{2}_{\delta}}||g||_{H^{2}}\leq c(C,B,C_{g}).

By Rellich-Kondrachov Theorem, the set
\{gf|f\in H^{2}_{\delta},||f||_{H^{2}_{\delta}}\leq C\}
is a compact subset of
L^{2}(B)
and therefore a compact subset of
L^{2}_{\delta+2}
.

Next, we prove invertibility. The classical Laplacian is already an isomorphism from
H^{2}_{\delta}
to
L^{2}_{\delta+2}
for
\delta\in(-3/2,-1/2)
and is therefore Fredholm of index
0
. Adding a compact perturbation does not modify the Fredholm index of the operator. Thus, the operator
L
is Fredholm of index 0. Consequently, the injectivity implies the invertibility.

To prove injectivity, we use the maximum principle for functions with Sobolev regularity. Let
f\in H^{2}_{\delta}
satisfy

Lf=0.

Since
g\geq 0
, we may apply the maximum principle (see Chapter 8 of
[
8
]
). For any set
\Omega
of finite radius
R
with smooth boundary,

\displaystyle\sup_{\Omega}f\leq\sup_{\partial\Omega}f|_{\partial\Omega},

\displaystyle\inf_{\Omega}f\geq\inf_{\partial\Omega}f|_{\partial\Omega},

where the boundary trace is well defined since
f
is continuous by the Sobolev embedding into Hölder spaces (Proposition
8.9
). Moreover,

\displaystyle|f(x)|\leq\frac{C||f||_{H^{2}_{\delta}}}{|x|^{\beta}},

for some
C>0
and for
0\leq\beta<\delta+3/2
with
\delta\in(-3/2,-1/2)
. As a consequence,

\displaystyle|\sup_{\Omega}f|\leq\frac{C||f||_{H^{2}_{\delta}}}{R^{\beta}},

\displaystyle|\inf_{\Omega}f|\leq\frac{C||f||_{H^{2}_{\delta}}}{R^{\beta}}.

Letting
R\to+\infty
, we deduce that
f
is identically
0
. This proves the injectivity of
L
and thus its invertibility.

Finally, to prove the inequality (
8.24
), we work by contradiction.

Since
L
is asymptotic to a Laplacian in the sense of
[
1
]
(for
q=4
), we have the following inequality (Theorem
1.10
of
[
1
]
) for every
f
in
H^{2}_{\delta}

\displaystyle||f||_{H^{2}_{\delta}}\leq C(||Lf||_{L^{2}_{\delta+2}}+||f||_{L^{2}(B_{R})}).

(8.25)

The constants
C
and
R
depend on
g
and
\delta
.
If the inequality (
8.24
) is false, then there exists a sequence
f_{n}
such that

\displaystyle||f_{n}||_{H^{2}_{\delta}}=1,

\displaystyle\lim_{n->\infty}||Lf_{n}||_{L^{2}_{\delta+2}}=0.

Since the sequence is uniformly bounded, there exists a subsequence that converges weakly to
f
in
H^{2}_{\delta}
and that converges strongly in
L^{2}(B_{R})
by Rellich-Kondrachov.

Testing against
\forall\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{n->\infty}\int{Lf_{n}\varphi dx}=0,

by assumptions, and

\displaystyle\int{(Lf_{n}-Lf)\varphi dx}=\int{(-\Delta f_{n}+\Delta f)\varphi dx}+\int{g(f_{n}-f)\varphi dx}\leq\int{-\Delta(f_{n}-f)\varphi dx}+||gu||_{L^{2}(\Omega)}||f_{n}-f||_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

which implies that

\displaystyle\lim_{n->\infty}\int{Lf_{n}\varphi dx}=\int{Lf\varphi dx}.

Thus,
Lf=0
and therefore
f=0
by injectivity. Now, using the inequality (
8.25
), for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that for
n\geq m

\displaystyle 1=||f_{n}||_{H^{2}_{\delta}}\leq C(\varepsilon+||f_{n}||_{L^{2}(B_{R})}).

Since
\lim_{n->\infty}||f_{n}||_{L^{2}(B_{R})}=||f||_{L^{2}(B_{R})}
, as the convergence is strong, we deduce that
f\neq 0
, which is a contradiction. Therefore (
8.24
) holds.
∎

8.5
Strichartz estimates

Definition 8.15
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
be the considered dimension. A pair
(q,r)\in(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star})^{2}
is said to be Strichartz admissible if

\displaystyle q\geq 2,

\displaystyle\frac{2}{q}\leq(n-1)\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{r}\right),

\displaystyle(q,r,n)\neq(2,\infty,3),

\displaystyle r<\infty.

Theorem 8.2
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
be the considered dimension. For
(q_{1},r_{1})
and
(q_{2},r_{2})
Strichartz admissible satisfying

\frac{1}{q_{1}}+\frac{n}{r_{1}}=\frac{1}{q^{\prime}_{2}}+\frac{n}{r^{\prime}_{2}}-2=\frac{n}{2}-\gamma,

(8.26)

then there exists
C>0
, such that, for any
f
solution to the Cauchy problem

\begin{cases}\Box f=j,\\
f(0)=f_{0},\;\partial_{t}f(0)=\dot{f}_{0},\end{cases}

(8.27)

we have

||f||_{L^{q_{1}}([0,T],L^{r_{1}})}\leq C(||j||_{L^{q^{\prime}_{2}}([0,T],L^{r^{\prime}_{2}})}+||f_{0}||_{\dot{H}^{\gamma}}+||\dot{f}_{0}||_{\dot{H}^{\gamma-1}}).

(8.28)

Proof.

From
[
33
]
and
[
19
]
.
∎

8.6
Product estimates

We recall the following Sobolev product estimate.

Proposition 8.16
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
be the considered dimension. Let
1<p<\infty
,
r,s\in\mathbb{R}
,
r,s<\frac{n}{p}
, be such that
r+s>\max\left(\frac{n}{p}-\frac{n}{p^{\prime}},0\right)
for
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
. If
p>2
, we additionally assume that either
f\in W^{p^{\prime},-r}
or
g\in W^{p^{\prime},-s}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle||fg||_{W^{p,t}}\leq||f||_{W^{p,r}}||g||_{W^{p,s}},

(8.29)

for
t=r+s-\frac{n}{p}
.

Proof.

From
[
35
]
.
∎

We now state two lemmas providing product estimates based on Strichartz inequalities.

Lemma 8.17
.

Let
f
and
g
be two functions defined on
[0,T^{\prime}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
for
T^{\prime}>0
.
For
\nu\in(0,1/2)
and
p=\frac{1}{1-\nu}
, the following inequality holds

\displaystyle||fg||_{L^{1}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{2})}\leq C||f||_{L^{p}([0,T^{\prime}],H^{1/2-\nu})}(||\Box g||_{L^{1}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{2})}+||g(0)||_{\dot{H}^{1}}+||\partial_{t}g(0)||_{L^{2}}).

(8.30)

Proof.

For
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
, we define
2<\alpha=\frac{3}{1+\nu}<3
and
\frac{1}{2}=\frac{1}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\beta}
. Then,

\displaystyle||fg||_{L^{1}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq||f||_{L^{p}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{\alpha})}||g||_{L^{p^{\prime}}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{\beta})}

\displaystyle\leq||f||_{L^{p}([0,T^{\prime}],H^{1/2-\nu})}||g||_{L^{p^{\prime}}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{\beta})}.

We set
(r_{2}=2,q_{2}=\infty)
, which is Strichartz admissible. We look for
p^{\prime}
such that
(\beta,p^{\prime})
is Strichartz admissible too and satisfies

\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{3}{\beta}=\frac{1}{q^{\prime}_{2}}+\frac{3}{r^{\prime}_{2}}-2=\frac{1}{2}.

This implies that
p^{\prime}=\frac{\alpha}{3-\alpha}
and so
p=\frac{\alpha}{2\alpha-3}=\frac{1}{1-\nu}
. Applying Strichartz estimates to
||g||_{L^{p^{\prime}}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{\beta})}
leads to the desired inequality.
∎

Lemma 8.18
.

Let
f
and
g
be two functions defined on
[0,T^{\prime}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
for
T^{\prime}>0
.
For
\theta\in(0,1/2)
,
p=2(1-\theta)
and
p^{\prime}=\frac{2(1-\theta)}{1-2\theta}
, the following inequality holds

\displaystyle||fg||_{L^{1}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{2})}\leq||f||_{L^{p}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{2})}(||g||_{L^{p^{\prime}}([0,T^{\prime}],H^{2})})^{\theta}(||\Box g||_{L^{1}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{2})}+||g(0)||_{\dot{H}^{1}}+||\partial_{t}g(0)||_{L^{2}})^{1-\theta}.

Proof.

First, Hölder inequalities yield

\displaystyle||fg||_{L^{1}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{2})}\leq||f||_{L^{p}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{2})}||g||_{L^{p^{\prime}}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{\infty})},

(8.31)

Using the Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality from Proposition
8.11
, we get

\displaystyle||fg||_{L^{1}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{2})}\leq||f||_{L^{p}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{2})}(||g||_{L^{p^{\prime}}([0,T^{\prime}],H^{2})})^{\theta}(||g||_{L^{p^{\prime}}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{r_{1}})})^{1-\theta},

(8.32)

where
r_{1}=6\left(\frac{1-\theta}{\theta}\right)>6
with
\theta\in(0,1/2)
. We set
(r_{2}=2,q_{2}=\infty)
and
p_{1}=p^{\prime}
and search for
(r_{1},p_{1})
Strichartz admissible with

\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{3}{r_{1}}=\frac{1}{q^{\prime}_{2}}+\frac{3}{r^{\prime}_{2}}-2=\frac{1}{2}.

This yields
p^{\prime}=\frac{2(1-\theta)}{1-2\theta}
so that
p=2(1-\theta)
. Applying Strichartz estimates to
||g||_{L^{p^{\prime}}([0,T^{\prime}],L^{r_{1}})}
leads to the desired inequality.
∎
